People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

I’m sorry, I know that probably doesn’t answer it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Is there something that happened in the area you're avoiding that might be causing your fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. I came in because I just can’t really make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore. I keep finding reasons to stay in the back instead, even though I know being up front is part of my job. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything… it just feels like I can’t do it right now.\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you in the area you're avoiding near the front windows or on that part of the street?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what it is. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but after what happened there, I just can’t get myself to go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore. I keep making excuses to stay in the back, even though I know that’s part of my job. I’m sorry if I’m not explaining it very well.\n\nDoctor: Did you think you were in danger of being hurt or killed when the upsetting event happened near the front windows or on that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. In the moment I know I felt really scared, but I don’t know if I was specifically thinking, like, “I’m going to die” or anything. I don’t want to overstate it. It just felt dangerous, I guess… if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happened near the front windows or on that part of the street that's causing you to avoid it?\n\nPatient: I guess… since then, I just can’t make myself go near the front windows, or that part of the street outside. I know that probably sounds silly, and I’m not trying to avoid work, but I keep finding excuses to stay in the back instead. Even when I know I’m supposed to be up front, I just kind of freeze up and try to get someone else to do it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not like a sudden episode that peaks really fast, at least not that I’ve noticed. I do get really uncomfortable sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s that kind of thing. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Like if I’m near it, I can’t relax or do my job normally. It’s more that just seeing that spot outside the windows makes me… I don’t know, I can’t make myself go over there anymore. I keep trying to act normal, but I end up making excuses to stay in the back of the store instead, even though I know being up front is part of my job. I’m sorry, I know that probably doesn’t answer it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Is there something that happened in the area you're avoiding that might be causing your fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. I came in because I just can’t really make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore. I keep finding reasons to stay in the back instead, even though I know being up front is part of my job. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything… it just feels like I can’t do it right now.\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you in the area you're avoiding near the front windows or on that part of the street?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what it is. I keep making excuses to stay in the back, even though I know that’s part of my job. So I end up making excuses to be in the back instead, even though I know that’s not really okay.\n\nDoctor: Is there something in particular that you're afraid might happen if you go near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m not sure I can put it into one specific thing. It’s more like if I go near the front windows or that same part of the street, I just freeze up and feel like I need to get away from it. So I keep making excuses to stay in the back, even though I know that’s part of my job and I probably shouldn’t be doing that.\n\nDoctor: Is there something outside the window or on that part of the street that you're afraid of?\n\nPatient: Not like a specific person or thing, I don’t think. It’s more that just seeing that spot outside the windows makes me… I don’t know, I can’t make myself go over there anymore. I keep trying to act normal, but I end up making excuses to stay in the back of the store instead, even though I know being up front is part of my job. I’m sorry, I know that probably doesn’t answer it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Is there something that happened in the area you're avoiding that might be causing your fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. I came in because I just can’t really make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore. I keep finding reasons to stay in the back instead, even though I know being up front is part of my job. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything… it just feels like I can’t do it right now.\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you in the area you're avoiding near the front windows or on that part of the street?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what it is. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but after what happened there, I just can’t get myself to go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore. I keep making excuses to stay in the back, even though I know that’s part of my job. I’m sorry if I’m not explaining it very well.\n\nDoctor: Did you think you were in danger of being hurt or killed when the upsetting event happened near the front windows or on that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. In the moment I know I felt really scared, but I don’t know if I was specifically thinking, like, “I’m going to die” or anything. I don’t want to overstate it. It just felt dangerous, I guess… if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happened near the front windows or on that part of the street that's causing you to avoid it?\n\nPatient: I guess… since then, I just can’t make myself go near the front windows, or that part of the street outside. I know that probably sounds silly, and I’m not trying to avoid work, but I keep finding excuses to stay in the back instead. Even when I know I’m supposed to be up front, I just kind of freeze up and try to get someone else to do it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not like a sudden episode that peaks really fast, at least not that I’ve noticed. I do get really uncomfortable sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s that kind of thing. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid thinking or talking about what happened near the front windows or on that part of the street?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, at least some. I don’t really like talking about it, because it makes me feel kind of embarrassed, like I should be able to just do my job. I mostly notice it with the front windows or that part of the street—I keep finding reasons to stay in the back instead. I’m not trying to make a big deal out of it, but I guess I am avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories or thoughts about what happened near the front windows or on that part of the street that come into your mind when you don't want them to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I try not to make it a big thing, but the crash kind of pops into my head when I’m not trying to think about it. Like if I’m setting up displays near the front, suddenly I can hear that bang again, and I see the glass coming down for a second like it’s happening right then. It’s… embarrassing, honestly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding the front windows and that part of the street outside?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess it’s been about two weeks now. Pretty much since the car came through the store window during that overnight shift. I know that probably sounds like kind of a long time to still be weird about it, but… yeah, about two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding the front windows and that part of the street outside been causing you any problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it is starting to mess with work. I had to leave in the middle of doing a window reset because I just couldn’t finish it. I felt really stupid about it.\n\nAnd now I’m kind of worried that I can’t handle the parts of my job that involve the storefront at all, which… I know is basically part of my job. Is that what you mean by causing problems?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more that just seeing that spot outside the windows makes me… I don’t know, I can’t make myself go over there anymore. I’m sorry, I know that probably doesn’t answer it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Is there something that happened in the area you're avoiding that might be causing your fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. I came in because I just can’t really make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore.
